## Appendix A: Glossary

The following terms recur across the book. Each entry gives a short definition and the chapter where the concept is most fully developed. Cross-references point to where the term is used most substantively elsewhere.

* * *

**Active-constructive responding (ACR).** Shelly Gable's framework for how partners respond to each other's good news. Active-constructive (engaged, specific, follow-up questions) is the only response pattern that consistently predicts relationship satisfaction. The other three patterns — active-destructive, passive-constructive, passive-destructive — fail to land. (Ch 6)

**Alloparent / alloparental support.** A caregiver other than the biological parents who is regularly involved in raising a child. In Sarah Hrdy's cooperative-breeding framework, humans evolved with multiple alloparents per child (extended kin, older siblings, grandparents, unrelated allomothers). Modern paid help (nannies, daycare workers) and chosen-family caregivers count as alloparents in the contemporary form. (Ch 11)

**All-or-nothing marriage.** Eli Finkel's term for the contemporary configuration in which a single partnership is asked to provide what was historically distributed across an entire ecosystem of relationships and institutions — friendship, romance, intellectual companionship, spiritual support, growth scaffolding, co-parenting, financial partnership. (Ch 9)

**Attachment.** In Helen Fisher's three-system framework, the oxytocin- and vasopressin-mediated neurochemical state of slow-built felt safety with a person who has become a secure base. Distinct from lust and from romantic love. (Ch 2) In Bowlby–Ainsworth attachment theory, the broader behavioral system regulating closeness-seeking and felt safety with caregivers. (Ch 3)

**Attachment cry.** Sue Johnson's EFT term for the deeper signal underneath most destructive surface conflicts — a question or plea about whether one matters to the other, whether the other is reachable, whether the bond is intact. Surface fights about prescriptions, dishes, or weekends are usually about the attachment-cry layer. (Ch 7)

**Attachment pattern.** A working model of how attachment-seeking signals will be received in a particular relational context. The four canonical patterns (secure, anxious, avoidant, disorganized) are corners of a two-dimensional space (anxiety × avoidance) rather than fixed personality types. Patterns are dimensional, partner-specific, and modifiable. (Ch 3)

**Bid for connection.** Any small action by one person — a comment, glance, touch, question, observation, gesture — that requests another's attention, acknowledgment, or engagement. Often brief and below the bidder's conscious awareness. Gottman identified bid response patterns as one of the most predictive variables in long-term relationship outcomes. (Ch 5)

**Co-regulation.** The process by which two nervous systems modulate each other's physiological state — heart rate, cortisol, threat-system activation — through ongoing physical, vocal, gestural, and emotional contact. The empirical foundation for the book's claim that relationships are physiological infrastructure. (Ch 1)

**Cooperative breeding.** Sarah Hrdy's framework: humans evolved as cooperative breeders, with offspring raised by multiple caregivers including extended kin and unrelated allomothers, not by parents alone. The modern nuclear family is, on this account, evolutionarily aberrant. (Ch 11)

**Destiny vs growth beliefs.** Raymond Knee's distinction between believing relationships work or don't on the basis of fundamental compatibility (destiny) versus believing relationships develop through effort and repair (growth). Destiny believers leave relationships earlier when conflict arises, on average; growth believers stay and repair. (Ch 3)

**Dual-control model.** John Bancroft and Erick Janssen's framework for human sexual response: two semi-independent neurological systems, the _sexual excitation system_ (SES, responsive to sexual cues) and the _sexual inhibition system_ (SIS, responsive to threats and distractions). Many cases of low desire reflect SIS over-activation rather than SES deficit. (Ch 8)

**Earned secure attachment.** The development of secure-pattern functioning in adulthood by people whose early childhood patterns were not secure. Mechanisms: sustained relationship with a securely-functioning partner, attachment-informed therapy, deep friendships at attachment depth, sustained engagement with mentors or communities whose relational norms differ from the family of origin's. (Ch 3)

**Erotic-domestic paradox.** Esther Perel's clinical observation that desire in long-term partnerships requires conditions (mystery, distance, autonomy, novelty, unattainability) that partly oppose the conditions building security (familiarity, closeness, merging, predictability, assured availability). (Ch 8)

**Four Horsemen.** John Gottman's term for the four conversational patterns that predict relational distress: criticism (attacking character rather than behavior), defensiveness (counter-attack or justification), stonewalling (physical or psychological withdrawal during diffuse physiological arousal), and contempt (communication of superiority). Contempt is the strongest single predictor of dissolution. (Ch 7)

**Lust.** In Fisher's three-system framework, the testosterone- and estrogen-mediated system motivating sexual contact. Non-specific (not directed at one individual), fluctuating, distinct from romantic love and from attachment. (Ch 2)

**Perceived partner responsiveness (PPR).** Harry Reis's construct: the felt-sense that one's partner _understands_ who one is, _validates_ that self, and _cares_ in ways responsive to that specific self. Possibly the single most predictive construct in contemporary relationship research. (Ch 6)

**Perpetual problems.** John Gottman's finding that approximately 69% of recurring couple disagreements are not solvable but rather to be lived with — disagreements rooted in fundamental personality, value, or family-of-origin differences. The healthy response is _managing_ rather than _solving_. (Ch 7)

**Repair attempts.** Small actions during conflict that try to lower temperature and reconnect — light remarks, self-deprecation, touches, deliberately softened tones, _let's take a break_. Gottman's deeper finding: lasting couples make many repair attempts and _receive_ each other's; dissolving couples either make fewer or fail to receive them. (Ch 7)

**Responsive desire.** Emily Nagoski's distinction (paired with _spontaneous desire_): desire that emerges in response to context — physical touch, emotional intimacy, deliberate setup — rather than arriving on its own. More common in older adults and in long-term partnerships; not pathological. The implication: action precedes desire, rather than desire precedes action. (Ch 8)

**Romantic love.** In Fisher's three-system framework, the dopamine-driven OCD-cocaine-pattern state of intense focused preoccupation with a specific person. Time-limited (months to a few years); biologically not sustainable indefinitely. Distinct from lust and from attachment. (Ch 2)

**Sexual fluidity.** Lisa Diamond's longitudinal finding that meaningful fractions of adults experience genuine shifts in the targets of their lust and romantic-love systems across adult life. Configurations are revisable; fixed-orientation models miss the variation. (Ch 2 §2.5)

**Social baseline theory.** James Coan's framework: the human brain evolved expecting cooperative others to be present, and the brain's default metabolic budget is calibrated to that expectation. Operating embedded in a cooperative group is baseline; operating alone is the metabolic deviation. (Ch 1)

**Spontaneous desire.** Sexual desire that arises seemingly out of nowhere — the lust system generating interest without specific external prompting. More common in younger adults and (statistically) in men. Partner of _responsive desire_. (Ch 8 §8.4)

**Strange Situation.** Mary Ainsworth's experimental protocol — separation, reunion, observation of how an infant uses the caregiver to regulate distress — that revealed the four canonical attachment patterns. (Ch 3)

**Suffocation model.** Eli Finkel's account of how the all-or-nothing marriage produces relational distress: the gap between expanded marital expectations and any single partnership's capacity to meet them registers as overall inadequacy, even in objectively strong partnerships. (Ch 9)

**Third places.** Robert Putnam's term (extended from Ray Oldenburg's coinage): spaces beyond home and work where adults regularly encounter others outside the immediate circle — cafes, religious institutions, civic associations, parks, community centers, hobby clubs. Their decline is a structural driver of contemporary loneliness. (Ch 10, Ch 14)

**Thread A.** The Harvard Study of Adult Development. Anchored in Chapter 4; revisited at Chapter 6 (PPR predicting cognition), Chapter 10 (Harvard data converging with Holt-Lunstad's), Chapter 13 (the marriage-vs-depth finding), Chapter 14 (Waldinger's TED-talk closing). The book's empirical anchor.

**Thread B.** The reader's two-week relational inventory. Introduced in Chapter 1 and applied in every subsequent chapter. The book's central practice: four lines per day across one to three close relationships, covering bids, responsiveness, repair, and unspoken needs.

**Thread C.** Modern conditions eroding relational ecosystems. The contemporary social arrangements (devices, geography, design, work, infrastructure, cultural narratives) that systematically undermine the relational mechanisms the framework identifies. Visited in every chapter, building toward Chapter 14's structural-political endpoint.

**Turning toward / away / against.** The three responses to a bid for connection. _Turning toward_ engages with the bid (even briefly); _turning away_ fails to register or respond; _turning against_ responds with hostility, irritation, or dismissal. Stable couples turn toward roughly 86% of bids in Gottman's data; divorce-trajectory couples turn toward roughly 33%. (Ch 5)

**2-AM tier.** The small set of people one would actually call at 2 AM in genuine distress, whose call would be answered warmly, whose presence one would actually want. For most healthy adults, the comfortable range is three to five people. A practical diagnostic for relational depth as distinct from social activity. (Ch 10)

**Working model (attachment).** A brain's expectation about how attachment-seeking signals will be received, built from accumulated experience and revisable by new experience. Distinct from a fixed personality trait. (Ch 3)

* * *

_Glossary entries are intentionally brief. For full development of each concept, see the chapter listed._
